oversight and data analytics. CIO viewpoints are developed for Bank of America Private Bank, a division of Bank of America, N.A., (“Bank of America”) and Merrill Lynch, Pierce, Fenner & Smith

The Global Wealth & Investment Management Investment Strategy Committee (“GWIM ISC”) is responsible for developing and coordinating recommendations for short-term and long-term
investment strategy and market views encompassing markets, economic indicators, asset classes and other market-related projections affecting GWIM.
BofA Global Research is research produced by BofA Securities, Inc. (“BofAS”) and/or one or more of its affiliates. BofAS is a registered broker-dealer, Member SIPC and wholly owned subsidiary

of Bank of America Corporation.
All recommendations must be considered in the context of an individual investor’s goals, time horizon, liquidity needs and risk tolerance. Not all recommendations will be in the best interest
of all investors.
Asset allocation, diversification and rebalancing do not ensure a profit or protect against loss in declining markets.
Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

ADVANCED ECONOMIES
EUROPEAN UNION / EUROZONE
Strength on multiple fronts. Business activity in September, mea -
sured by the composite PMI, reached its highest level since April 2023
(53.1, +1.1pt). This was led by a strong improvement in the services
index (53.0, +1.4 pt) while the manufacturing PMI was stable and

comfortably in expansion (52.7). Input and output price indices both
rose sharply, especially services output prices (56.1, highest since Fe -
bruary 2024) indicating the energy price shock is spreading. New car
registrations in the EU rose 4.5% y/y in August and 5.3% year-to-date
in August. Electric cars’ market share continues to rise (to 27.7% of
new registrations year to date). Less positively, bank loans to corpo -
rates halved in August compared to July. On the trade front, the EU

has adopted rare provisional safeguard measures (quotas and tariffs)
against a type of industrial steel products, in response to a recent
surge in imports from China. It also reached a “substantial agree -
ment” on a new trade deal with The Philippines. As part of its efforts
to safeguard the role of the euro in DLT-finance, the ECB has launched
its new 'Pontes' project, which is a first step toward providing a digi -
tal euro for wholesale uses. Pontes creates a link between the TAR -

GET2 interbank settlement system in central bank money and private
DLTs. The ECB also announced it intends to purchase a limited amount
of tokenised government bonds through its own balance sheet, to
gain hands-on expertise in the digital asset ecosystem.
Upcoming: September European Commission Economic Sentiment In -
dex (Tuesday).
ECB leadership race kick-started. While ECB watchers have been on
tenterhooks for an official announcement by President Lagarde of an

early departure (her term ends in October 2027), the news came from
ECB Board member Isabel Schnabel announcing she would leave her
role early, on January 3rd, 2027, to take on the role of Financial Coun -
sellor at the IMF. The Eurogroup of finance ministers is now expected
to take a package approach to choosing successors for the President,
as the Board seat left vacant by Ms. Schnabel and the Chief Econo -
mist post is due to become vacant in May. The final decision on the

Presidency rests with European Leaders. Country and gender balance
considerations will be paramount.
France. Back to expansion, but mixed signals. The flash composite
PMI returned to expansion for the first time since November 2025,
reaching 51.2 in September after 48.5 in August. The improvement is
driven by services, at 51.4 (+3.4 m/m), while the manufacturing PMI
fell slightly to 50.3 (from 51.1 in August). Insee's business climate,
however, fell back to 96 in September from 98 in August (100 = long-

term average). It was weighed down by services, retail and wholesale
trade. The business climate was stable in the manufacturing sector
(101) and construction (96). Both surveys concur on the fact that
selling price pressures are increasing in the manufacturing sector.
Consumer confidence was stable for the 3 rd consecutive month, at 86
in September (20-year average: 93). The government has announced
an increase in fuel subsidies amounting to EUR 450 million. This mea-

sure is not expected to deteriorate further the fiscal deficit (projected
by the government at 5.4% of GDP in 2026).
Upcoming: September Inflation (Wednesday); Draft budget law and
final manufacturing PMI (Thursday)
Germany. Business activity and sentiment improve further. The com -
posite PMI for September was above expectations by rising to 53.8
(+2 pts m/m), its highest level in 11 months. This was driven by the
services sector returning to growth after five months of contraction

(52.9; +3.2 pts m/m). Growth in the manufacturing sector leveled
off slightly but remains robust. The production index stood at 55.9
(-0.8 pt m/m). Companies are reporting further signs of resilience,
with an acceleration in production, stable outlooks, rising employ -
ment in services, and stabilisation in manufacturing. Price pressures
are elevated and, in most categories, rising. Meanwhile, the Sep -
tember Ifo index of business climate rose to its highest since May

2023 (89.9; +1.2pts). Expectations for business development in the
next 6 months rose to an 11-month high of 90.4 (+1.4pts). All sectors
saw improvements in their indicators. Consumer confidence deterio -
rated further, however: the GfK index deteriorated to -30.6 in October
(-3.8 pts m/m, its lowest since last May), driven by a significant drop
in income expectations. Still, the decline in consumer sentiment was
partially mitigated by a fifth month of improving economic expecta -

tions.
Upcoming: August retail sales, September unemployment rate and
flash inflation (Wednesday).
UNITED KINGDOM
Rate hike increasingly likely, as indicated by the Bank of England’s
Governor and two Deputy Governors (owing to the persistence of the
energy price shock). Resilient activity points in the same direction:
September PMIs showed continued expansion (composite fell slightly
to 51.7), with services slowing down (51.7; -0.8 m/m), and manufac -

turing accelerating (to 52, +0.3 m/m) due to resilient demand in AI
and defence. Business optimism held steady at a six-month high and
manufacturing confidence reached its strongest point since Februa -
ry on robust investment and new projects, offsetting a slight dip in
services caused by geopolitical concerns. GfK Consumer Climate unex-
pectedly rose to -13 in September (+1 m/m), hitting its highest level
since August 2024 due to improved personal finance and economic

outlooks.
Upcoming: Q2 GDP Final (Thursday); BoE DMP Firms Price Expecta -
tions survey (Friday).
UNITED STATES
A ‘good news is bad news’ week. PMIs are typically little watched in
the US owing to low correlation to GDP , nevertheless this week they
were a key trigger for a brutal selloff in US yields across the curve,
featuring 20+ basis point moves and a very weak 5Y bond auction.
Hawkish Fed speakers talk also contributed (Barr, Williams, Paulson).

The preliminary September composite PMI reading surged to 58.4, a
five-year high, defying expectations of a drop. Both the Services and
Manufacturing PMIs rose to multi-year highs. New orders, employ -
ment, and input prices all rose sharply, with input price growth ac -
celerating. Core capital goods orders (non-defence, ex-aircraft) rose
1.6% m/m in August, well above expectations, after an upwardly re -
vised +0.6% m/m in July — extending the momentum in business

equipment investment. The US 30-year yield rose to 5.53%, its highest
since 2004. The 10Y touched 5.23%. 30-year mortgage rates are at 7%.
Upcoming: GDP final estimate for Q2, August PCE report (Wednes -
day); September ISM manufacturing (Thursday); September non-
farm payroll/employment report (Friday).

UNITED STATES / CHINA
Just a trade truce extension. On the eve of the Trump-XI Summit,
the United States and China have extended their trade truce by two
months, until 10 January. This highlights both their willingness to
avoid new trade tensions and their inability to find a durable agree -
ment on most key topics. Outstanding trade issues include Chinese

purchases of US agricultural products, rare-earth supplies and tariff
reductions on bilateral trade. Following the Leaders’ Summit, where
AI and Iran were also discussed, the trade ministers announced a
deal to apply a low tariff regime to $30bn of non-sensitive goods
imported by each side.
JAPAN
No more (need for) reflationism. This stance was endorsed by both
the ministers of Finance and Economic Growth on Friday 25 th, as
Prime Minister Takaichi told President Trump in New York that the

weak Yen was a concern. The Yen strengthened, by 1.2% on the same
day, to 157 per dollar. This should leave the BoJ freer to tighten mo -
netary policy at the needed pace (we expect 2 more hikes by March
2027). Its underlying inflation indicator — CPI excluding fresh food
and special factors — rose to 2.6% y/y in August from 2.3% in July,
while business activity remains in steady expansion: the Composite
PMI registered its 18th consecutive month of expansion in September,

falling slightly to 52.5 (-1 pt m/m). Strong external demand for se -
miconductors, AI, and defence offset less dynamic domestic demand,
especially in services. Cost pressures are moderating but remain high.
Upcoming: August industrial production and retail sales (Wednes -
day), TANKAN survey (Thursday).
EMERGING ECONOMIES
Buoyant debt. Hard currency debt issuance is on track for another re -
cord year, after an already strong 2025, despite the rising global rates

environment. $190 bn have been issued over January-August, about
USD30bn more than over the same period last year. Qatar and Saudi
Arabia even returned to the market this month.
Central Banks divergence. Central banks kept monetary policy on
hold in Hungary (at 5.50% after three consecutive rate cuts, with in -
flation at 1.3% y/y in August), Indonesia (at 5.75% despite renewed
depreciation of the rupiah, -2% against the USD since the beginning
of September), Mexico (at 6.50%, for the third consecutive time), and

Morocco (unchanged at 2.25% despite low inflation at 0.3% on ave -
rage in the first 8 months of the year). Meanwhile, monetary autho -
rities hiked the benchmark rate in South Africa (by 25bp for the se -
cond time since the start of the Iran war, to 7.25%. It occurred despite
softer-than-expected inflation in July, at 4.4% y/y), and cut in Nigeria
(by 350bp, to 23%. CPI inflation has been broadly stable since the
start of the year, averaging 15.5% y/y over January-August).

Hungary. Back on the EU integration path. The Central Bank an -
nounced the inflation target will be gradually reduced to 2.5% from
2028 vs. 3% currently, in line with the objective to adopt the euro in
the medium term. Meanwhile, the European commission has pro -
posed to unlock EU funds worth EUR 4.2 bn (1.8% of GDP) in light of
progress in the rule of law; it is still subject to the council’s vote.
ENERGY
Crude oil. Prices rose slightly over the week (106.7 USD/b Monday,

28th morning, +6.4% w/w) as ongoing negotiations did not provide bet-
ter prospects for the oil market. The gap with physical barrel (Dated
Brent) remains quite large (18.7 USD/b on Friday). On a better note,
KSA has restarted its East-West pipeline, which should progressively
recover its full transit capacity (4mb/d).
Diesel. US export restrictions? With Diesel prices reaching all time
highs in the US (near USD 6.5/gal) six weeks away from the mid-term

elections, the Trump administration has indicated it is considering a
diesel export ban. Experts believe a ban would at best provide short-
term relief to diesel prices in parts of the US, so it is not a foregone
conclusion that export restrictions will be adopted. Most industry
leaders and European leaders have been pleading against them (the
US contributes to 40% of EU imports of diesel). In Europe, French
President Macron has urged for a release of strategic reserves and

regulatory changes to increase refining capacities in the near term.
Given its critical role throughout the economy (in farming, transpor -
tation and some industrial usages), high diesel prices have a parti -
cularly broad stagflationary impact. US crack spreads declined by
10% w/w (to 98.2 USD/b), but the European benchmark declined as
well (-0.2% w/w to 91.6 USD/b), thanks to adequate inventories.
European gas benchmark. Stable this week (to 73.8 EUR/MWh) on the

back of an acceleration in EU stock refiling (70.9% of full capacity for a
target at 80% by end-October).

ECONOMIC WEEK AHEAD: September 28 - October 2

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
ECONOMIC WEEK AHEAD:
September 28 - October 2
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 27 Sept 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Monica Sauro / Unsplash

### Page 3

The week ahead is jam-packed with labor market data releases, capped
off by September's employment report (Fri). August's PCED and the third
estimate of Q2 GDP arrive Wednesday, along with BEA's annual
revisions, followed by ISM's M-PMI on Thursday.
FedSpeak continues, with Richmond Fed President Tom Barkin, Governor
Lisa Cook, Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee, Minneapolis Fed
President Neel Kashkari, and New York Fed President John Williams

among those scheduled to speak. They will likely weigh in on September’s
FOMC rate-hike decision and the recent rise in global bond yields.
Overseas, the Reserve Bank of Australia will issue the week's only
scheduled interest-rate decision. China's official PMIs and flash Eurozone
inflation figures are also due out, with European Central Bank President
Christine Lagarde making several public appearances. Micron reports
earnings on Wednesday.
Here’s more:

(1) Employment. September's employment report (Fri) is the headliner.
Payrolls rose 162,000 in August, lifting the three-month average to 71,300
(chart). Private payrolls accounted for 127,000 of the gain, led by leisure &
hospitality (62,000) and goods-producing industries (41,000). We expect a
figure close to 100,000 for September.
Fed Chair Kevin Warsh said at his September 16 press conference that
the unemployment rate, at 4.1% in August, is "basically running consistent

with full employment." He added, "I don't believe that we need to do harm
to the labor markets to achieve our [inflation] objective."

### Page 4

Challenger's September layoff announcements (Thu) follow August's
52,900, still low by historical standards (chart). Layoffs probably remained
light last month, according to initial unemployment claims, which held at a
four-week average of 203,600 as of September 18. Warsh noted that
claims are running at levels consistent with full employment.
August's ADP private payrolls rose 38,000, below the 47,000 consensus
and July's upwardly revised 46,000. September's ADP report (Wed) may

show improvement, with ADP's weekly readings rising for three straight
weeks to a four-week average of 20,000, up from a late-July bottom of
8,250 (chart). That pace equates to roughly 85,000 a month.

### Page 5

July's JOLTS data showed job openings at 7.3 million, with the share of
consumers saying "jobs are plentiful" at 27.0% in August, both consistent
with a stable labor market (chart). We expect more of the same in
August's JOLTS report (Tue).
(2) GDP. The third estimate of Q2 GDP (Wed) follows the second estimate
of 1.5% saar, with the GDP price index up 6.4%. It arrives with BEA's
annual update, which will revise prior data. The Atlanta
Fed's GDPNow model estimated Q3 growth at 5.0% as of September 25,

led by an 18.5% jump in business equipment spending (chart).

### Page 6

(3) PCED. August's core PCED (Wed) follows July's 3.3% y/y. That was
well below August’s 4.6% pace of core PPI final demand for personal
consumption (chart). The core CPI was 2.4% in August. Based on the CPI
and PPI data for August, Warsh estimated August’s core PCED at about
3.2% y/y and headline PCED at about 3.6% y/y. The Cleveland Fed's
Inflation Nowcasting model projects hotter readings of 3.4% and 3.8%,
respectively. (The model’s m/m rates are 0.34% and 0.27%.)

(4) M-PMI. ISM's M-PMI (Thu) was 54.6 in August, with the NM-PMI at
55.4 (chart). S&P Global's flash M-PMI for September jumped to 57.0 from
53.9, suggesting another strong ISM reading. The flash composite rose to
58.4, the strongest since July 2021. Input costs across goods and
services rose at the fastest pace since October 2022.

### Page 7

S&P 500 forward earnings continue to rise, increasing 37.2% y/y in
September, suggesting more upside for the M-PMI (chart).
(5) Earnings. Micron reports fiscal Q4 results (Wed) for the quarter ended
in August. Based on analysts’ consensus estimates, they expect revenue
of $50.8 billion, up from $11.3 billion a year ago. EPS is expected at
$31.45, within management’s guidance range of $30.00-$32.00 and up
from $3.03 a year earlier. Yet the stock has fallen 11.1% since peaking

after its last quarterly earnings report back in June (chart).

### Page 8

Analysts expect Micron's net income margin to widen to 70.7% in fiscal Q4
from 69.6% in Q3. Its forward profit margin has reached 71.6%, well
above its 2018 peak around 42% (chart).

Document 272: Eurozone _ Growth on track despite the turbulence (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Eurozone _ Growth on track despite the turbulence (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

8
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
GROWTH ON TRACK DESPITE THE TURBULENCE
Growth was stronger than expected in Q2 and is set to strengthen further in the coming quarters. According to our fore-
casts, it will reach 1.5% in 2027 after 1.1% in 2026. This growth is expected to be driven by investment and its knock-on
effect on exports. On an annual average basis, inflation is expected to stand at 3% in 2026 and 2.8% in 2027, peaking at

the end of 2026. The ECB is expected to continue its monetary tightening. At this stage, a single further rate rise, aimed at
keeping inflation expectations anchored, is expected in December. The impact of rising interest rates (key policy rates and
market rates) on public finances and private sector borrowing conditions will be worth monitoring.
GROWTH: BEYOND THE SHOCKS, A RESILIENT MOMENTUM
Growth in the Eurozone remained robust in the first half of 2026, at

0.25% q/q in Q1 and 0.33% q/q in Q2, excluding Ireland (where economic
activity is volatile). It was driven by Germany, where signs of a recovery
are becoming clearer (see Germany). Our baseline scenario forecasts
growth of 1.1% in 2026, followed by 1.5% in 2027 on an annual average
basis. This growth is expected to be driven by the strengthening of
trends already underway in public and private investment (particularly
in defence and AI) and in exports (trade in services and intra-zone trade

in goods). Household consumption is likely to be resilient too. According
to our forecasts, job creation would continue to underpin household
incomes, thereby cushioning the impact of inflation.
A NEW LEASE OF LIFE FOR INDUSTRY
The Eurozone is undergoing one of the most profound industrial trans-
formations in its history. This is giving rise to divergences between key
sectors but, above all, to new drivers of growth whose effects are beco-
ming increasingly apparent (see our analysis). Indeed, the signs of an

economic recovery are growing stronger. Business sentiment indicators
show a marked improvement in the economic outlook across most sec-
tors (Chart 2), driven primarily by electronics (AI, digitalisation), the
automotive sector and aerospace. European industry is thus benefi -
ting from strong demand for capital goods; a structural trend that will
continue to be fuelled by ongoing investment cycles in AI, defence and
electrification. The introduction of minimum European content quotas

in the draft law on the industrial accelerator, together with the Com -
mission’s intention to tighten the criteria for access to European public
procurement markets, will provide additional support for European in-
dustry in the longer term.
FOREIGN TRADE: A REPOSITIONING THAT IS PAYING OFF
Following a decline in 2026, linked to rising energy import costs, the
trade surplus is expected to rise to around 3.0% of GDP in 2027 (of
which 2% of GDP for goods and 1% for services). Growth in European ex-

ports has slowed compared with its pre-Covid trend and is being ham-
pered by the decline in Chinese and US markets. However, Eurozone
exports in volume rose significantly in Q2 (+3.4% q/q).
More importantly, since Liberation Day (April 2025), Eurozone coun -
tries have found significant sources of growth amongst other European
partners, which has enabled them to cushion the decline in exports to
certain third markets. This is evident in exports to the rest of the EU,

EFTA countries (Switzerland in particular) and, to a lesser extent, the
United Kingdom, Ukraine and the Balkans (Chart 3). The momentum of
intra-European trade therefore remains very strong. It is expected to
strengthen further with the anticipated investments in defence, AI and
electrification.
FINANCING: FINANCIAL CONDITIONS ARE EXPECTED TO REMAIN TIGHT
Financial conditions in the Eurozone remained tight in Q2 2026, accor-
ding to indices calculated by the ECB (including the BIG [Broad Inter -

mediation Gauge]). They are expected to tighten more significantly in
the second half of the year: i/ the rise in long-term bond yields, which
has accelerated since the outbreak of the conflict in Iran, is expected to
continue; and ii/ the ECB will continue its monetary tightening (+25 bps

in Q3, then in Q4). For the time being, the debt burden is still weighing
only moderately on public finances due to the long average maturity of
countries’ debt (8.2 years on average in the Eurozone in July), but this
burden will increase by 2030.
On the corporate front, the rise in interest rates has remained contained
(+2 bps since January 2026, reaching 3.9% in July), which has contri -

buted to the strong growth in new investment loans (cumulated over
one year, +9.1% y/y in July compared with 15.3% y/y in September 2025).
By contrast, cumulative net debt securities issues over one year re -
mained stable in July, whilst cumulative net issues of listed shares over
one year halved month on month.
Rising interest rates will continue to hamper new variable-rate
home loans to households (around 65% of new lending) more signi -
ficantly than fixed-rate loans, for which rates stood in July at 3.69%

(+4 bps m/m) and 3.36% (+2 bps m/m) respectively. New home loans,
which are stable in 2026 on a cumulative over one year basis, are there-
fore unlikely to see any improvement. A fall in real-estate transactions
could weigh on new consumer credit through a reduction in expenditure
on home furnishings and renovations, whereas consumer credit has
been fairly buoyant so far (year-to-date, +5.2% y/y in July compared
with +1.4% y/y in January).
LABOUR MARKET: RESILIENCE YET TO BE CONFIRMED

Employment in the Eurozone rose again by an average of 0.1% q/q in
the first half of 2026, driven by Spain and Portugal. Given the growth
outlook in these two countries, this momentum is likely to continue,
whilst business sentiment surveys in Germany suggest that employ -
ment is beginning to stabilise. Nevertheless, employment levels are
plateauing (France, the Netherlands) or even falling in other countries
(Austria, Finland), which is likely to slow the decline in the Eurozone

unemployment rate, meaning it is expected to remain close to current
levels in 2027. Furthermore, second-round effects of the rise in inflation
on wages cannot be ruled out: the ECB’s wage tracker suggests wage
growth of close to 3% by summer 2027 (2.9% y/y in June 2027, compared
with 2.6% in September).
INFLATION: A PEAK EXPECTED IN LATE 2026
Changes in wage dynamics will be a key factor in the inflation out -
look for 2027, whilst energy price rises are picking up again, driven

by the conflict in the Middle East. This rise is even sharper than in
the spring for gas (linked to restocking ahead of winter) and is likely
to feed through to electricity prices in several countries. We therefore
anticipate inflation peaking at 3.7% y/y in Q4 2026, before easing as the
energy component falls. On an annual average, headline inflation is
expected to stand at 3% in 2026 and then at 2.8% in 2027.
At this stage, the pass-through of rising energy prices to other com -

ponents of the consumer price index, particularly services, remains li -
mited. The alternative core indicators monitored by the ECB, including
the PCCI (Persistent and Common Component of Inflation, 2.1% in July),
remain close to the 2% target. Core inflation, however, is expected to
peak two months later than headline inflation (3% in March 2027).
Inflationary risks are nevertheless tilted to the upside. These are lin -
ked to food prices, which are expected to be affected by the energy

shock and weather conditions (summer drought, El Niño); they could
also stem from a delayed impact of energy price rises on underlying
inflation. The rise in prices of energy-dependent inputs (notably che -
micals and plastics) and those linked to the AI sector is expected to be
sustained. Growth, which we expect to remain robust over the coming
quarters, will also fuel underlying inflationary pressures.
MONETARY POLICY: TIGHTENING WITHOUT SLOWING (GROWTH)

Following two 25-basis-point rises (in June and September), we ex -
pect a third in December, which would bring the deposit facility rate
to 2.75%. It would then be in slightly restrictive territory (we estimate
the upper bound of the neutral rate to be 2.5%). This rise would be
aimed primarily at anchoring inflation expectations and, consequently,
at curbing the pass-through effects of inflation. Monetary tightening
would therefore remain limited. Indeed, the overshoot of the inflation

target would be temporary, and the operational framework adopted by
the ECB in June 2025 allows it to tolerate this, provided it remains brief.
However, it cannot be ruled out that the ECB might take further action
if the decline in inflation proves slower than expected.
PUBLIC FINANCES ARE CONTINGENT ON RISING INTEREST RATES
The fiscal impulse in the Eurozone is expected to be clearly positive in
2026 due to the rise in defence and infrastructure spending in Germany.

This will contribute to a widening of the country’s primary deficit (from
+0.9 pp of GDP to 2.8%, according to our forecasts). The improvement in
public finances is expected to stall in both France and Italy, at least in
2026, due to the impact of inflation on the debt burden via index-linked
bonds. This trend is expected to reverse in 2027 with less expansionary
fiscal policies. Nevertheless, the increase in the debt service burden
would fuel a further rise in the public deficit, which would thus reach

around 3.3% and 3.5% of GDP in 2026 and 2027 respectively (compared
with 2.9% in 2025). Against this backdrop, the Eurozone’s public debt-
to-GDP ratio is projected to exceed 90% of GDP in 2026 and reach 93%
of GDP in 2027, according to our forecasts.
Article completed on 15 September 2026

Document 286: German Defense_ An Ecosystem in Transformation (2026-09-25)

September 25, 2026 
This is the first section of a report available to clients via our Deutsche Bank 
Research site here. 
Launching Private Company Analysis with the German Defense Sector 
Traditional analysis that focused on public companies overlooked a critical 
market segment: the many important competitors that are privately owned, 
typically by families or private equity firms. To address this, we are launching 
dedicated analysis of Europe’s private-market giants, starting with Germany, 
where a traditionally dominant private sector means large firms outnumber 
public ones almost two to one. We begin with the German defense sector, where 
understanding the private ecosystem is essential: it is the source of critical 
innovation, the solution to supply-chain issues, and the venue for a future wave 
of M&A, consolidation, and IPOs. 
From Incremental Modernization to Industrial Scaling 
Germany's defense landscape is undergoing its most significant transformation 
in decades, shifting from a paradigm of 'incremental modernization' to 'industrial 
scaling' . Triggered by the Zeitenwende in 2022, the new era is underpinned by 
substantial financial commitments, including a €100bn special fund and 
constitutional reforms enabling sustained long-term spending. The objective is 
no longer merely to fill discrete capability gaps but to execute a full 'ecosystem 
build-out' that addresses structural choke points and enhances industrial 
capacity. This transformation is creating a more resilient and scalable German 
Defense Industrial Base (DIB), prepared to meet surging domestic and European 
demand. 
Technology, Integration, and the Rise of New Players 
This strategic pivot is also reshaping the industrial and technological 
environment. EU initiatives (EDF , EDIRPA, ASAP) are fostering joint funding and 
integrated supply chains, while the market is experiencing a 'next wave' of 
technological change, with a clear shift in value towards drones, software-
defined systems, electronic warfare, and AI-driven capabilities. These dynamics 
force industry consolidation but also create openings for agile start-ups, which 
are vital for innovation, as speed and software become paramount. This report 
provides an in-depth primer on this evolving sector. We identify Germany’s 
industrial strengths and technology gaps, analyze the key private players poised 
to benefit, and examine the structural themes shaping the market - from funding 
dynamics and procurement reforms to technology roadmaps.

### Page 2

25 September 2026
Aerospace & Defence
Defense
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
Half the Industry is Invisible
We are Changing that.
Equity research has always looked at the listed half of the economy. That half is
shrinking. Companies stay private for longer, the largest capital pools are now
private, and in Germany the listed universe was never the industry to begin with.
There are roughly 120 listed German companies with sales above €1bn - and
roughly 200 unlisted ones.  In our home market, large private companies

outnumber public ones almost two to one.
That gap is not a curiosity. It is an analytical problem.
If a sector's innovation, its supply-chain frictions, its pricing behavior and its
eventual consolidation sit largely outside the listed universe, then covering only the
listed names means covering only part of the argument. With this report we start
private-market giants, beginning in Germany.
Why we are starting with German defense
Defense is the sharpest version of the problem we are trying to solve. Germany is

in the middle of its largest rearmament cycle since reunification, but much of the
capability being bought is not primarily produced by the listed defense groups. It is
produced by an ecosystem: family-owned system houses that have supplied the
Bundeswehr for decades, venture-capital-backed companies that did not exist
three years ago, and the tier-2 and tier-3 suppliers who sit on the actual constraints
- energetics, seekers, qualified electronics, test capacity, cleared labor. That

ecosystem is where the innovation happens, where the supply chain either breaks
Understanding it is not a side-project to covering Rheinmetall, Hensoldt or Renk. It
is a prerequisite.
Executive Summary - Eight Points Carry this Report
1.  The spending shift is structural, not cyclical. The €100bn special fund was
the headline; the 2025 constitutional reform exempting defense spending
above 1% of GDP from the debt brake was the substance. Government

budgets now foresee more than €150bn of annual defense spending by
2029, against roughly €52bn of core budget in 2024. NATO's 2025 pledge
of 3.5% of GDP on core defense by 2035 puts a floor under the back-end.
2.  Budgets are not revenue. Between an approved budget and a delivered
capability sit parliamentary approval for anything above €25m, tender
design, contracting, qualification, test capacity and acceptance. The
February 2026 procurement acceleration reform helps, but the conversion

chain - not demand - sets the delivery slope.
3.  Value is moving to software, autonomy and effects. Drones, software-
defined systems, electronic warfare and AI-enabled decision layers are
taking share from platform-centric spend. That favors companies whose
iteration cycle is measured in weeks, and it is the clearest reason private
and venture-capital-backed firms now sit inside mainstream force design.
4.  Production is deterrence - and production is where Germany is short.

Scaling breaks at specific points: energetics permits, seekers and
guidance electronics, qualification burden, specialized labor, and limited
or scales.
closing that gap. We are launching dedicated, ongoing analysis of Europe's

### Page 3

25 September 2026
Aerospace & Defence
Defense
Deutsche Bank AG Page 3
test windows. Almost every one of those nodes is occupied, at least in part,
by a private company.
5.  The private ecosystem is now procurement-relevant, not just innovation-
relevant. The February 2026 Bundeswehr loitering-munitions framework,
awarded to Helsing and STARK from an initial package of roughly €540m,
is the clearest proof point. Sovereign buyers are contracting with

companies two to five years old.
6.  Scale still sits with the incumbents. Diehl generated €5.4bn of sales and
€703m of EBIT in FY2025, with Defence at roughly €2.3bn; Rohde &
Schwarz passed €3bn of revenue in FY2024/25 with no bank debt. These
are not start-ups with a defense angle - they are among Europe's more
significant defense industrial assets, and they happen to be family-owned.
Regulation is the binding constraint, in both directions. Export licensing,

foreign investment screening, NATO interoperability and compliance
intensity limit scaling and exits. But policy also accelerates - fast-track
procurement, EU joint programs, state-backed vehicles for defense tech.
Why defense, in numbers
The money. Germany's core defense budget has moved from roughly €47bn in
2021 to a projected path above €150bn by 2029, with the €100bn special fund
bridging the middle of that ramp. The 2025 debt-brake reform matters more than

the fund itself: by exempting defense spending above 1% of GDP, it converts a one-
off program into a sustained baseline. Procurement approvals have already
responded: 97 major defense projects approved in 2024 against 55 in 2023, with
over €58bn allocated.

The conversion chain. What is in doubt is throughput. Contracts above €25m
require binding Bundestag budget-committee approval, and tendering, supplier
orders, integration, testing and acceptance each add time. Europe's fragmentation
compounds it: European states operate around 179 different major weapon
systems against 33 in the US, and 19 different main battle tanks against one.

Between 2020 and 2024, European arms imports rose 155%, and European NATO
members sourced 64% of those imports from the US, up from 52% in 2015-19.
Whether 'buy European' becomes policy rather than rhetoric is the single largest
swing factor for the domestic industrial base.
Figure 2: Budget to Output: The Conversion Chain
Source : Deutsche Bank research
Where the private sector sits. The constrained nodes are consistent across almost
every program: energetics capacity, defense-qualified electronics, test and

certification, and cleared labor. Private companies are disproportionately present in
exactly those nodes. Diehl's acquisition of Dynamit Nobel was, in substance, the
acquisition of explosives-related operating authorizations it describes as unique in
Germany. STARK's dependence on TDW for German-manufactured warheads is
the same story one tier down. Rohde & Schwarz occupies the secure-
communications and SIGINT/EW layer no platform program can route around.

Where the new money is going. Germany attracted close to €1bn of defense and
security venture-capital funding in 2025, second only to the US. The deal mix since
2021: unmanned systems (34 deals), surveillance and space (24), other defense
tech (24), electronic warfare (10) and cyber (8). Helsing raised $1.8bn at an $18bn
valuation in July 2026; STARK Defence raised €500m in June 2026 above €3.5bn.

Capital of that scale changes the industrial map.

The five debates - and where we go next
1.  Can Germany convert budget into capability fast enough?  Demand is
settled; throughput is not. Watch acceptance capacity, qualification

timelines and framework design rather than headline budgets.
2.  Do the newcomers industrialize, or stall at the factory door?  Helsing,
STARK Defence, ARX Robotics and Quantum Systems are all making the
same transition from software and prototypes into serial hardware. That is
where defense start-ups historically fail. The next 18 months are the test.
3.  Do incumbents get disrupted, or absorb? The likelier outcome is a denser
partner network - Diehl with Hensoldt and Airbus, Helsing with Saab and

Kongsberg, ARX Robotics with RENK and Daimler Truck. Partnership maps
are becoming a better guide to competitive position than product catalogs.
4.  Does 'buy European' become real? A conscious substitution of even part
of the 64% US import share would be a structural repricing of the European
industrial base. Fragmentation and interoperability standards cut against
it.
5.  Who is financed, and how? Rising capital intensity, thin private disclosure,

constrained exit routes under foreign-investment screening, and uneven
ESG treatment will all influence the financing and consolidation wave.

### Page 6

25 September 2026
Aerospace & Defence
Defense
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
The Argument in Five Parts
1. The Strategic Landscape: Russia's 2022 invasion of Ukraine, combined with the
distancing of the US as Europe's protector, represents a turning point for the safety
of Europe and its defense industry. The shift in policy is creating immense demand
across Europe for arms in the decades to come, leading to significant M&A and
investing opportunities. German companies are uniquely suited to serve the rising

demand due to their immense technical know-how and their already-existing
industrial sector. Combined with targeted funding programs, these developments
both stimulate existing companies and incentivize start-ups that push the technical
frontier forward.
2. New Wars Change the Old Battlefield: Recent wars in Ukraine and the Middle
East have reshaped modern warfare, shifting capital from traditional contractors to
start-ups that can keep pace with rapid innovation cycles. Key trends defining this

new era include: unmanned systems creating a transparent battlefield that
demands adaptability; huge munitions consumption proving that production itself
is deterrence; the battlespace expanding into commercial space and cyberspace;
and societal resilience against disinformation becoming a vital security element.
This all occurs amid a new great power competition, heightening the risk of multi-
theater wars and the use of nuclear threats to back conventional aggression.

3. How to Gain Exposure – Investment Opportunities in Private Gems:  Rapid
innovation in modern warfare is creating significant investment opportunities in
Germany's defense sector, as start-ups outperform slow, traditional procurement.
This operational shift is fueled by a long-term surge in European defense spending
targeted at R&D and equipment. Investors can gain exposure through key 'private
gems', from established firms like Diehl Group and Rohde & Schwarz to high-

growth start-ups like Helsing (AI) and Quantum Systems (drones). These start-ups
leverage a mix of venture capital and government grants with innovative business
models, making the German private defense market a compelling investment area.
4. Trend – Europe Buys European & Europe Buys between Middle Powers: Recent
energy and security shocks have shattered Europe's post-Cold War assumptions,
creating an urgent push for strategic self-reliance amid uncertainty over the long-

term US security guarantee. This pivot is evident in new security partnerships, such
as Germany deepening defense ties in the Indo-Pacific (Japan) and North Atlantic
(Canada). Paradoxically, Europe's rearmament has increased its reliance on US
hardware (64% of imports '20-'24) and its defense sector remains highly
fragmented. The core challenge is whether Europe's quest for autonomy will lead
to a 'buy European' policy; doing so would unlock a monumental internal market,

driving consolidation and creating a truly independent defense capability.
5. The Regulatory Landscape & Investment Constraints: The defense industry is
uniquely constrained and regulated, and investors must understand its landscape.
Growth is not driven simply by demand but is filtered through government budgets,
procurement laws, and export controls. This creates risks: budgets don't translate
to immediate revenue, market access can be denied politically, and production

cannot be scaled freely. High compliance costs and national security reviews that
limit exit strategies add further complexity. Successful investment, therefore,
requires mastering these political and regulatory realities, not just identifying
demand.

### Page 7

Deutsche Bank Research Institute

Executive summary
For a near term, we see the strong data center AI
GPU/TPU/ASIC/CPU capex and deployment to support HBM, DDR5,
NAND ﬂash demand. However, starting 4Q26 and beyond, we have
started to see the pricing hike was/is decelerating and gross margin
is topping but capex growth is accelerating. Despite months of
inventory MOI and capital density are still at a healthy level, we
believe Micron is turning itself from gross margin expansion to

capacity expansion mode. Like Nvidia in the past 12 months, no
matter how good the ﬁnancial numbers are ahead of analysts’
estimates, Micron might continue to beat the market on numbers
but rest (consolidate up/down) on share price performance in line
with the change of Sox index.

與部署，仍有⼒地⽀撐著 HBM 、 DDR5 及 NAND 快閃記憶體的需求。然⽽，展

峰值，⽽資本⽀出增速卻在加快。儘管庫存⽔位（以季度庫存量 /MOI 衡量）及

(raised from 16 in 2Q26) covering 35% of sales throughout

Risks for us to monitor: Till now, we believe Micron and

of 87% for 3Q26. We thus see limited upside on ASP to boost
sales and limited upside on gross margin to boost EPS for the
next few quarters. In addition, by raising capex to boost bit
growth and sales growth might result in depreciation cost
increase to aﬀect the gross margin improvement.

Westpac New Zealand Economics

Michael Gordon, Senior Economist | +64 9 336 5670 | michael.gordon@westpac.co.nz      Connect with us
Key results
• The economy grew by 0.2% in the June quarter. That was in line with our expectation, and
slightly above the median market forecast of 0.1% (range of -0.3% to +0.3%).
• It was also above the flat outturn that the RBNZ expected in its September Monetary Policy
Statement (which didn’t have the benefit of the final data releases that led us and the market

to upgrade our forecasts last week).
• Lingering seasonality in the data means that growth tends to be understated in June
quarters (and overstated in March quarters). Adjusting for this suggests an underlying
growth pace of around 0.5% over recent quarters.
• The New Zealand economy appears to have held its ground through the US-Iran conflict,
though it hasn’t been completely unscathed. The areas where the impact was most
apparent were in travel and hospitality, as the surge in fuel prices led households to tighten

their belts on discretionary spending.
• Elsewhere, it was perhaps more a question of what might have been – we’ve seen the
economy picking up momentum since the second half of last year, but the headwinds from
sharply higher fuel prices mean that we haven’t seen a further acceleration.
• On an annual basis, GDP was up by 2.6%, comfortably outpacing population growth of 0.7%.

### Page 3

Westpac New Zealand Economics
3
GDP by production
• The production measure of GDP , generally regarded as the more reliable of the two, rose by
0.2% in the June quarter. This followed a 0.9% rise in the March quarter (revised up from
0.8%, largely due to updated data on building activity).
• While the overall result was in line with our forecast, it was a little weaker than expected in
the details. The ‘market’ sectors of the economy surprised us to the downside on balance,

while there were surprisingly large gains in central government administration (up 2.1%) and
healthcare (up 0.8%).
• As expected, the largest positive contribution came from construction (up 2.7%), with
growth in both residential and non-residential building. Residential building consents have
risen strongly over the last year, but this has only recently started to translate into a higher
level of building work.
• There were also solid gains in manufacturing, wholesaling, information and

telecommunications, and rental and real estate services.
• The sectors that saw the largest declines were transport and hospitality, most likely
reflecting the impact of the sharp rise in fuel prices.
• The seasonal distortions in the GDP data were captured by the large negative ‘balancing
item’ for the quarter, which subtracted around 0.2% from the reported growth rate.
Employment
• The number of people employed rose by 0.2% in the March quarter, according to the

Household Labour Force Survey (HLFS). Employment was up 0.4% compared to a year ago.
• The HLFS result was in line with the 0.2% rise in filled jobs from the Monthly Employment
Indicator (MEI), a comprehensive measure based on income tax data. While there are some
differences in coverage for these two measures, any divergence between the two is most
likely to reflect sampling error in the HLFS.
• The employer-focused Quarterly Employment Survey (QES) was similarly positive, recording

a 0.7% rise in full-time equivalent workers and 0.6% gains in filled jobs and hours paid.
• All of these measures are broadly consistent with our forecast of a 0.8% rise in GDP for the
quarter (which we expect will be partly boosted by seasonal distortions).

### Page 4

Westpac New Zealand Economics
4
GDP by expenditure
• The expenditure measure of GDP rose by 0.4%, slightly more than the 0.2% rise in
production. (We don’t produce a separate forecast of the expenditure measure, due to large
gaps in data availability.)
• Household consumption was around flat for the quarter. Spending on goods was up, but
there was a sharp fall in spending by NZ households overseas, as a sharp rise in airfares
discouraged overseas travel. This also meant that imports of services were less of a drag on

GDP for the quarter.
• Residential investment saw a strong 4.4% rise, as was also reflected in the GDP production
figures for the construction sector.
• Business investment was mixed, with a strong lift in non-residential buildings, plant and
machinery, and intangibles (which includes software). However, there was a 9% drop in
purchases of transport equipment.
• Government consumption (which covers services like health, education, law and
order etc) fell by 1.7%. This measure has been – and still is – running higher than what

the Government’s fiscal accounts would suggest. This may be reconciled by future
data revisions.
• Goods exports surged by 4.9% for the quarter. This likely reflects the timing of shipments,
with much of this being met by a rundown of inventories, though there was also a solid lift in
dairy production volumes.

### Page 5

Westpac New Zealand Economics
5
International comparisons
• The 0.2% rise in quarterly GDP was towards the lower end of the range of our peer countries,
but the seasonal distortion in the New Zealand figures means that this comparison is not
appropriate. On an annual basis, GDP growth of 2.6% was slightly higher than the median,
and ahead of Australia’s growth of 2.1%.
• In per capita terms, New Zealand’s GDP is slightly above pre-Covid levels. While that puts

us in the lower half of the pack, the broader picture is effectively ‘the US vs the rest’, with
strong productivity gains seeing US growth take off in recent years, leaving other developed
economies in a relatively tight cluster.
• New Zealand had one of the largest post-Covid bounces in GDP , fuelled by substantial
monetary and fiscal stimulus. This also left us as one of the most overheated economies by
2022, requiring a significant tightening in monetary policy to bring inflation under control.

• The decline in interest rates from mid-2024 to late 2025 was an important factor in seeing
per-capita GDP stabilise and gradually trend higher again over the last couple of years.

Michael Gordon, Senior Economist | +64 9 336 5670 | michael.gordon@westpac.co.nz      Connect with us

### Page 6

Westpac New Zealand Economics
6
Implications
• Our current forecasts are for a solid lift in activity over the second half of this year, with 0.6%
growth in Q3 and 0.8% in Q4. While there is nothing in today’s data that would warrant a
change in these forecasts, the ongoing Middle East conflict and the resurgence in fuel prices
suggests downside risks to activity in the months ahead.
• Our GDP Nowcast model, which incorporates the latest high-frequency data, suggests

around 0.5% growth in Q3 GDP , a little below our current forecast. This estimate
will evolve as more data arrives, with upcoming business confidence surveys being
particularly important.
• The better than expected Q2 result, along with the upward revision to Q1, suggest that the
economy isn’t operating as far below full capacity as the RBNZ assumed in its September
MPS forecasts. These results may help to assuage some of the policy committee’ concerns

about downside risks to growth. But we think they will have more of an eye towards the
evidence on pricing behaviour, as well as the rapidly evolving Middle East situation.
• Our view remains that the next OCR hike is more likely to come at the December review. An
October hike can’t be ruled out, but the RBNZ has signalled a strong preference to wait and
see how the 50bp of tightening to date plays out.

2
日本の金融政策見通し：（井上）
米国の金融政策見通し（井上）
＜メインシナリオ（需要主導のインフレ圧力へ
シフト）：緩やかな利上げ（確率60％）＞
 FRBは、9月の見通しで、経済が力強さを増したと
評価した一方、インフレ率の2％目標への収斂に
時間を要する点を懸念
 FOMCメンバーは2026年中にも う1回の利上げを
見込む一方、2027年中は1回の利上げと現状維
持ないし利下げに2分。この間、市場は合計3回を
超える利上げを織り込み
 2026年中は、既往のエネルギー価格上昇の国内
への波及リスクに対して12月に1回の利上げを行
い、2027年には需要主導のインフレ圧力を抑制す
る目的でもう1回の利上げを行い、政策金利（FF
レートの誘導目標）は4.25～4.5％へ
 その後は、政策金利が明確に引き締め領域に入
るだけに、FRBも経済や物価の反応を見極めるた
め、政策金利を現状維持
＜代替シナリオ②（インフレ圧力が早期に沈

静化）：利上げを停止（同15％）＞
 エネルギー価格の迅速な低下によって、供給要因
による国内物価への波及が迅速に収束
 需要主導のインフレ圧力は残存するが、2026年12
月の利上げによって4％台に達する政策金利によ
り十分引き締め的と判断して現状維持に転換
 資産価格のオーバーシュートとその後の急速な調
整によって、経済の下方リスクがむしろ増大
 基調的インフレのアンカーがポイント
＜メインシナリオ（基調的インフレ率が目標に
収斂）：政策金利の速やかな中立化（確率
60％）＞
 日銀は、9月利上げの際に、経済の底堅さに自信
を示す一方、基調的インフレの上振れリスクを意識
 植田総裁は、基調的インフレを2％近傍に収斂させ
ることを主眼とする「新たな局面」に入ったとの認識
 本年12月にさらに25bpの利上げを行い、年末には
1.50％。2027年3月にも追加利上げで政策金利は
1.75％へ到達
 ただし、政策金利が明確に中立化する中で、経済
活動への負荷が徐々に明確になる可能性

 日銀によるビハインドザカーブの懸念が後退する
中で、長期金利の焦点は財政運営（消費減税の財
源等）や米国の長期金利による影響へ
＜代替シナリオ①（インフレ圧力が想定以上
に継続）：利上げを継続（同25％）＞
 景気が底堅い下で、エネルギー価格の高止まりに
よる国内物価への波及が強まりつつ継続
 インフレ期待を抑制するため、FRBは2027年中に
利上げを継続し、2027年末に5％近傍へ上昇
 経済や物価への影響だけでなく、金融環境のタイ
ト化が非直線的に出現するリスク
＜代替シナリオ②（経済の回復が想定以上に
遅延）：利上げの減速（同10％）＞
 輸出の好調が内需に波及せず、経済活動が停滞
 円安の抑制も必要であるため、日銀はより緩やかな
ペースの利上げにシフト
 日米金利差の拡大による円安圧力が輸入インフレ
→実質購買力低下→一層の内需停滞を招くリスク
＜代替シナリオ①（エネルギー価格の高騰が
想定以上に継続）：金融引き締めの領域へ（同
30％）＞
 エネルギー価格の高止まり、ないし円安の継続に

よって国内物価への波及が想定以上に長く継続
 基調的インフレの上振れを阻止するため、日銀は利
上げを継続し、ターミナルレートは2％台前半に上昇
 経済と物価のトレードオフが深刻化し、政府の経済
政策との調整が困難化

＜ポイント＞
 足下では台風や長雨等による悪天候や中東情
勢悪化によるコスト高が逆風の中 、AIブームによ
る米国経済の堅調さ 、実質賃金の緩やかな改善

 米国経済の緩やかな回復が続く中 、食料品の消

＜リスク＞
 中東情勢の予想以上の悪化により 、原油高が一

 高市政権の積極財政による景気上振れ。ただし、

＜ポイント＞
 米国経済は個人消費と 設備投資を牽引役に堅調持
続（上図）。個人消費は株高の資産効果が背景
 金利上昇で住宅市場は悪化。一方、潤沢なキャッ
シュフローを背景にAIデータセンター投資は活況
 足元の株式市場はAI企業の利益成長が主導する
「業績相場」と、金利上昇でPERが下がる「逆金融相
場」が併存。綱引きで株価には停滞感出るも、長期
的には名目GDPに沿って上昇継続の公算（下図）
 大統領2年目の株価は、中間選挙が与党不利と なり
やすく、政策停滞懸念から軟調傾向。3、4年目は大
統領選に向けた政策テコ入れで騰勢が強まる傾向
＜リスク＞
 金利急騰やAIブームの腰折れで株安と景気後退
 イラン紛争の長期化によるスタグフレーション
 インフレ急進とFRBの大幅利上げで景気後退

単位労働コスト
（３四半期先行、左軸）
日本物価見通し（宮嶋）
米国物価見通し（渡辺）
＜ポイント＞
 関税や中東情勢に伴うエネルギー高によりコア財
物価が上振れ（上図）
 長期インフレ予想は安定しており、賃金・サービス
物価への二次的波及は限定的。エネルギー高が
ホームメード・インフレに発展するリスクは限定的
 労働生産性の上昇が単位労働コストを抑え 、基
調的な物価を抑制する見込み（下図）

 供給要因のインフレは27年前半に剥落へ。ただし、
堅調な米景気がディマンドプル ・イ ンフレを 招き、
27年以降も物価は2％目標に着地しない公算
＜リスク＞
 AI関連の需要増で電子部品や電力の価格を中心
にインフレ上振れ
 AIによる生産性向上のディスインフレ圧力
 イラン紛争早期終結で油価下落、インフレ下振れ
 トランプ大統領がFRBの独立性を脅かし、金融政
策の信認と物価抑制効果が低下する恐れ
 政府債務増大とドル価値の低下に伴う インフレ
＜ポイント＞
 現状の物価は政府の支援策により上値が抑えら
れている格好（上図）。同対応策の終了を想定す
れば、中東情勢悪化によるインフレ圧力により 、
コアCPIは26年度末に3％近傍に加速する公算
 今後の中東情勢の小康状態を想定し 、食料品の
消費減税による下押し効果もあり 、27年度のコア
CPIは減速に向かう と 予想。28年度以降は減税
効果剥落により 再び加速へ。減税効果を除けば 、
物価は2％目標圏内で推移すると予想

 長期インフレ予想は足下 2％程度（下図）。今後
は中東情勢悪化による影響剥落や物価目標達
成への信認から目標圏で推移と予想
＜リスク＞
 原油高や財政悪化懸念、それに伴う 円安が再度
進めば物価上振れリスク大
 食料品の消費減税に関わる政策の詳細が不透
明で、物価への影響も 不確実性が大きい
注：コアCPIは生鮮食品除くベース
インフレ率は2027年以降も2％目標に着地しない公算

市場予想
（左軸）
（期待政策金利、％）
日本の金利見通し（宮嶋）
米国の金利見通し（渡辺）
＜ポイント＞
 日銀はインフレ上振れ防止のため利上げをペー
スアップ 。先行き は、12月・来年3月に利上げ予
想（金融政策の頁参照） 。その後の政策金利は
基調物価の2％前後の推移の下で横ばいを想定
 長期金利は 、利上げペースアップ等から 上昇に
歯止め。ただし 、米金利高や日銀国債保有減少
等から、3％近傍の高水準継続と予想（上図）
 超長期金利は世界的な財政懸念等が逆風も、発

行減額等への期待から小幅に低下へ （下図）
＜リスク＞
 想定以上のイ ンフレ加速と なれば、政策金利が
2％を超え、イールドカーブ上方シフトのリスク
 グローバルな財政懸念や高市政権の日銀利上
げ牽制姿勢が強まれば 、長・超長期ゾーンの金
利上昇が加速（スティープ化）
＜ポイント＞
 長期金利＝期待政策金利（政策金利予想）＋ター
ムプレミアム（国債保有リスク）（上図）
 タームプレミアムの上昇要因は、トランプ政策を巡
る 不確実性から、財政不安や国債の需給悪化懸
念、中間選挙後の不透明感へシフト。AI企業の大
量起債に伴う投資家資本の争奪戦も一因
 期待政策金利が上昇。イ ラ ン 紛 争 に 伴 うコ ス ト プ ッ
シュ・インフレと 堅調な経済指標がFRBを タカ 派化さ
せ、市場の利上げ期待を押し上げる（下図）
＜リスク＞
 イラン紛争早期終結で原油安、金利低下
 インフレや財政悪化、トランプ氏のFRBに対する政
治圧力等による「悪い金利上昇」
 ウォ ーシュFRB議長のフォワードガイダンス曖昧化

で、金融市場のボラティリティが上昇
 FRBの資産圧縮の加速でタームプレミアム急上昇
 金利上昇による景気後退の結果、金利下振れ

ドル円の見通し（尾河）
＜ポイント＞
 前四半期から全体的にドル円の長期見通しを
小幅ながら円高方向に修正
 今年の円安トレンドのテーマであった日銀の
「利上げの遅れ（Behind the Curve）」は後退
 日米の利上げは、共にあと2回程度と 予想。日
米の金利差が著し く開く、或いは縮小はせず、

今後1 年程度は155 円を 中心とし た上下10円
程度のボックス相場が続くと 予想
 一方で、今後デジタル赤字がさらに膨らむ可
能性など 、構造的な円安要因は残るため （下
図）、その後は緩やかな円安トレンドが継続
＜リスク ＞
 日本の財政懸念が再燃することにより長期金
利の上昇を伴った円安が加速するリスク
 トランプ政策による米財政懸念により 、国債価
格、株価、ドルのトリプル安が進むリスク
 米中間選挙後も 、トランプ大統領の 「ドンロー
主義」による諸外国との摩擦が続き 、米国の
信認低下を伴うドルの大幅安が進行するなど
【日米実質金利差（米－日・10年）とドル円】
【日本のサービス収支】

ユーロの見通し（森本）
2025 2026 2027 2028 2029
ユーロドル 1.10 1.12 1.13 1.17 1.20
ユーロ円 163 179 185 191 198
＜ポイント＞
 中東情勢を受けたエネルギー価格の上昇と
物価見通し の上方修正を背景に、ECBは3年
ぶり の利上げを実施。ただユーロ圏景気は低
成長が続いており、追加利上げ余地は限定的
 ユーロドルは長期的には独米実質金利差と の
連動性を維持 。当社は26/12と 27/3の米利上
げを 予想し ており、金利差の観点から来年前
半までは上値が重い展開を予想（上図）
 その後は米利上げ局面の一巡に加え 、ドイ ツ
の国防・インフラ支出拡大 （下図）による景気
押し 上げから 金利差は縮小へ。ユーロ圏の経
常黒字も 下支えとなり、27年後半以降はユー

ロの緩やかな上昇を見込む 。独国防省予算
は27年以降大幅な増額が計画されている
＜リスク ＞
 エネルギー高の長期化やフランスの財政不安
再燃により、景気悪化や域内の信用不安が強
まれば、ユーロが大きく下振れる恐れ

連邦軍特別基金の合計。2027年は政府予算案、2028～30年は財政計画。名目GDPは2025
年実績を起点に、連邦政府の名目GDP成長率見通しから算出

ソニーフィナンシャルグループ 金融市場調査部・研究員紹介
尾河 眞樹 （おがわ まき）
執行役員（金融市場調査部担当）
チーフアナリスト
ファースト ・シカゴ銀行 、JPモルガン ・
チェース銀行などの為替ディーラーを経
て、ソニー財務部にて為替リスクヘッジと
市場調査に従事。 その後シティバンク銀
行（現SMBC信託銀行） で個人金融部門
の投資調査企画部長として 、金融市場
の調査・ 分析を担当 。2016年8月より当
社執行役員。テレビ東京「Newsモーニン
グサテライト 」、日経CNBCなどにレギュ
ラー出演し、金融市場の解説を行ってい
る。主な著書に 『為替ってこんなに面白
い！（2024年幻冬舎新書） 』、『〈最新版〉
本当にわかる為替相場 （2023年日本実
業出版社）』などがある。ソニー銀行株式
会社取締役、 ウェルスナビ株式会社顧
問。人生投資アカデミー® 学長。
井上 哲也 （いのうえ てつや）
金融市場調査部
シニアフェロー エグゼクティブエコノミスト
日本銀行で主として国際金融市場やそ

れに関する政策、 2回の役員秘書（ 福井
副総裁、植田審議委員）、IT革命の影響
に関する研究等を行った後、 野村総合
研究所で主要国の金融政策 、デジタル
通貨、地域金融の活性化等に関する調
査を行い 、成果を多数の報告書等の形
で公表。2026年4月より現職。テレビ出演
や経済メディアへの寄稿を行っているほ
か、主な著書に 『異次元緩和 （2013年日
経BP）』、『デジタル円（2020年日経BP）』、
『CBDCとステーブルコインの衝撃（ 2026
年日経BP）』がある。1985年東京大学経
済学部卒、 1992年イェール大学大学院
経済学修士。
渡辺 浩志 （わたなべ ひろし）
金融市場調査部長 チーフエコノミスト
1999年に大和総研に入社し 、経済調査
部にてエコノミストとしてのキャリアをス
タート 。2006年～2008 年は内閣府政策
統括官室（経済財政分析 ・総括担当）へ
出向し、『経済財政白書』等の執筆を行う。
2011年からはSMBC 日興証券金融経済
調査部および株式調査部にて機関投資

家向けの経済分析 ・情報発信に従事。
2017年1月より当社 。内外のマクロ経済
についての調査 ・分析業務を担当。 ロジ
カルかつデータの裏付けを重視した分析
を行っている。
石川 久美子 （いしかわ くみこ）
金融市場調査部 シニアアナリスト
商品先物専門紙での貴金属および外国
為替担当の編集記者を経て、2009年4月
に外為どっとコムに入社し 、外為どっとコ

月から同社研究員として、外国為替相場

ト執筆の他、 テレビ東京 「Newsモーニン
グサテライト」など多数のメディアに出演
し、金融市場の解説を行う 。資源国・ 新
興国通貨に強い。著書に『円安はいつま
で続くのか 為替で世界を読む （2025年
マイナビ新書 ）』がある。Xでの情報発信
（@KumiIshikawa_FX）も行っている。
森本 淳太郎 （もりもと じゅんたろう）
金融市場調査部 シニアアナリスト

みずほフィナンシャルグループにて企画
業務、法人営業などを経験した後、 2019
年8月より現職 。外国為替市場の調査・
分析業務、 特にユーロやポンド 、スイス
フランなどの欧州通貨を専門に担当。 テ

トワイド 」、日経CNBC 「朝エクスプレス」

宮嶋 貴之 （みやじま たかゆき）
金融市場調査部 シニアエコノミスト
2009年にみずほ総合研究所（ 当時） 入
社以来、一貫してエコノミストとしてマク
ロ経済調査を担当。 2011年～2013年は
内閣府（経済財政分析担当）へ出向。官
庁エコノミストとして 『経済財政白書』 、
『月例経済報告』 等を担当。 2021年4月
より現職。2025年から景気循環学会・ 理

台加盟の影響と展望』（国立台湾大学出
版中心）、『激震 原油安経済』（日経BP）。
日本経済・債券市場、中国などのアジア
情勢が専門、セクターは不動産 、観光、
半導体等を分析。

Understanding the balance
ﬁlm industry a for larger AI
chip (Part I)
應該是時候了解 AI ⼤晶⽚抗翹曲應⼒平衡膜產業鏈
ANDREW LU ON GLOBAL SEMI/TECHS
SEP 28 ∙ PAID
READ IN APP
Executive summary
雖然  Balance Film 產業還在初始冒芽階段，但當 AI GPU ， TPU ， ASIC 越來

越⼤顆， ABF 載板及 RDL 處理層數越來越厚，越來越多不同熱膨脹係數晶⽚及
材料被封裝在⼀起，防翹曲技術及抗翹曲應⼒平衡膜就越來越重要。當未來 10
年， AI 算⼒數據中⼼的投資將受限於可⽤資⾦，可⽤電⼒，可⽤⼟地，可⽤晶
⽚產能，除了發展困難度超⾼的衛星軌道數據中⼼，提供更⾼算⼒ / 更低耗能
（單位⾯積算⼒耗能⽐較，增加每單位數據中⼼⾯積的 GW 算⼒）的⼤型 AI 訓
練及推理晶⽚的騰籠換⿃原廠升級（舊機櫃換成新機櫃 + 光通訊  scale
out/scale across ），可能反⽽是⼀個可⾏的⽅向。
What is the balance ﬁlm?
Balance Film （抗翹曲應⼒平衡膜）在先進封裝領域中，是⼀種專⾨⽤來「抵
消」異質整合製程中因熱膨脹係數（ CTE ， Coeﬃcient of Thermal
Expansion ）不匹配所產⽣之應⼒，進⽽抑制載板與晶圓翹曲（ Warpage ）
的關鍵材料。
在進⾏  CoWoS, FOPLP （⾯板級扇出型封裝）或玻璃基板（ Glass

Substrate ）等⼤尺⼨封裝製程時，矽晶粒、矽 / 玻璃中介層， ABF 載板、玻璃
基板（ Glass core substrate) 、銅線路（ RDL ）以及環氧樹脂（ EMC ）的膨
脹與收縮率各不相同。經過⾼溫製程冷卻後，這種應⼒失衡會導致嚴重的翹曲
變形，造成後續微影對位偏移或基板破裂。 Balance Film 透過貼合或塗佈於

### Page 2

基板特定區域，在前段製程中主動形成反向拉扯的「對沖」應⼒，將基板維持
在平整狀態。
When the market started to use balance ﬁlm?
我們需要了解，⼤型  AI GPU/TPU 晶⽚  的  CoW 矽 / 玻璃中介層「有翹曲問
題」不代表現在都⼀定會⽤獨⽴的  Balance Film 。  Balance Film 是⼀種額
外的「反向應⼒補償材料」，主要在⼤尺⼨、⾼層數、薄型化、 RDL/PLP/ 先進
封裝製程翹曲變嚴重時導⼊，⽽不是標準必備層。
Source: Andrew Lu on global semis/techs
Why a larger AI chip needs it?
Chip size matters
AI GPU/TPU 的矽中介層  Silicon Interposer 將⾛到 5.5x 光罩尺⼨，晶⽚⾯
積增加後，當台積電 AI GPU 客戶 Nvidia/AMD 及 AI ASIC/TPU 客戶亞⾺遜 / ⾕
歌 /Meta/ 微軟為了追求更⾼算⼒從 2028 年開始推疊邏輯電晶體及 12 顆

HBM4E 存儲記憶體，甚⾄ 20-24 顆 HBM5E 存儲記憶體，⼀⽚ 12“ 晶圓能切出
8 ⽚的 5.5x reticle size (70mmx70mm=4,900mm2) 晶⽚⼤⼩所需要的矽
中介層（ Silicon Interposer) ，將只能切出 5 ⽚  9x reticle size (90mm x

### Page 3

90mm=8,100mm2) 晶⽚⼤⼩的矽中介層，甚⾄ 4 ⽚  14x reticle size
(110mm x 110mm=12,100mm2) 的矽中介層，這意思是指假設台積電客
戶全⾯推出新⼀代  9x/14x reticle size 的晶⽚產品，即使數量不變，台積電
矽中介層的產能會⽴刻減少 40-50% ，如果再考量良率因素，矽中介層產能減
少 50% 或以上， 9x/14x reticle size 的矽中介層漲價⼀倍是⾮常合理的。因
為這個結構性變化，我們認為台積電對於幫助⼤晶⽚客戶採⽤玻璃中介層取代
矽中介層，於 2029 年由圓轉⽅加速擴張 CoPoS 產能及量產  CoPoS 將是台積
電未來 10 年不得不為的重⼤決定（請參考我們於 2026 年 3 ⽉ 27 ⽇發出的深度
報告：CoWoS/FOWLP 轉  CoPoS/FOPLP 圓轉⽅的典範轉移及產業鏈分析）。
現⾏⼤型  AI ABF substrate 已普遍需要  low-CTE core 、 symmetrical

stack-up 等⼿段抑制  warpage 。當矽 / 玻璃中介層未來超過  90mm x
90mm=8,100mm2 ⼤⼩，矽晶⽚、矽 / 玻璃中介層，銅（ RDL ）、與環氧樹
脂（ EMC ）之間的熱膨脹係數（ CTE ）差異，會導致翹曲（ Warpage ）呈現
「⾮線性」的急遽惡化。當翹曲變形量超過微影設備的對焦容許度（ Depth of
Focus ）或造成晶圓 / ⾯板破裂時，傳統的物理載具已無法壓制，就必須在製程
前段導⼊  Balance Film 。
Source: TSMC
付費訂閱戶可分享  Part I 全⽂，為何 AI ⼤尺⼨晶⽚需
要 BF(Size, layers, mix matters) ，貼在哪裡，是半
導體產品材料還是製程耗材？ Balance ﬁlm 移除後如
何防翹曲？ Part II 重點
Subscribed
Layers matter for ABF substrate

### Page 4

AI GPU/TPU I/O 數量暴增，因此  ABF 載板的  routing layer 越來越多。 ABF
載板本⾝是  build-up dielectric ，以後⾼階  ABF 載板  可達超過  20+
layers 。
Top 頂端
ABF / Cu
ABF / Cu
ABF / Cu
──────── core
ABF / Cu
ABF / Cu
ABF / Cu
Bottom 底端
⽬前理想狀態是上下完全對稱，內部應⼒互相抵消，但實際  AI ABF 載板，因
為不同的  power plane, routing density, copper density,
interposer/RDL 只在⼀側， HBM/GPU/TPU 集中在頂端不可能永遠完全對
稱， molding/underﬁll/TIM 熱介⾯材料（ TIM ， Thermal Interface
Material) 等材料不同，因此熱循環後會產⽣  bow / twist 。 Balance Film 的

作⽤就是⼈為加⼊⼀個相反⽅向的  stress ，使整體  stack 的  bending
moment 接近零。
Source: TSMC
Mixed chips matter
未來的 AI GPU/TPU 已經從單純  AI 加速器  + ABF substrate 變成  AI 加速器
+ CPO/Optics on 中介層  (EIC/PIC/FAU) + 矽 / 玻璃中介層  + HBM 的超級
複合體，⽽每種材料的熱膨脹係數都不同：

### Page 5

- 矽：約  2.6 ppm/K
- 銅：約  16.5-17.0 ppm/K
- 玻璃：約 0.5-10 ppm/k
- ABF 載板：平⾯⽅向  X-Y 軸  12-16 ppm/K ；垂直⽅向  Z 軸  30-50 ppm/K
所以從室溫到  reﬂow 250°C 左右，應⼒差⾮常⼤。 Ajinomoto 也把  low
CTE / high Tg 列為最新  ABF 材料的重要特性，⽬的之⼀就是控制熱機械變
形。
Source: TSMC
Where balance ﬁlm attached to?
Balance Film 不是留在最終晶⽚產品的材料，更像是製程耗材，核⼼概念是
「反向對沖」。因為封裝結構的正⾯（ Front-side ）堆疊了⼤量會產⽣應⼒的
材料，所以  Balance Film 通常是貼附在矽 / 玻璃中介層或玻璃載體  glass
carrier 的背⾯（未來也有機會⽤在  ABF 載板 ) ，並不等於 CoPoS 的專屬材料
（請參考之前圓 CoWoS 轉⽅ CoPoS 深度報告）。

根據不同的製程階段，其貼合位置與作⽤對象略有不同：
1. RDL （重佈線層）製程階段
貼合位置：  玻璃載體或矽 / 玻璃中介層的最底層背⾯，並可能隨著 RDL 處理層
數增加⽽多次使⽤。當  Silicon Interposer 經過背⾯研磨薄化⾄  50–100 微
⽶時，結構已無法⾃我⽀撐，必須暫時鍵合在玻璃載具（ Glass Carrier ）
上，請參考下圖。此時， Balance Film 就會改貼在「玻璃載具的最下⽅」，防
⽌巨型載板在後續製程中因上下⼒矩失衡⽽碎裂。

### Page 6

作⽤機制：  在載板正⾯不斷塗佈  PI/PBO 介電層與電鍍厚銅線路時，這些材料
在⾼溫固化與冷卻後會產⽣強⼤的收縮⼒，導致載板向上捲曲（ Smile 變形）
或向下凸起（ Cry 變形）。貼在背⾯的  Balance Film 會透過⾃⾝特製的⾼分
⼦材料特性，在同溫層產⽣「反向的拉扯應⼒」，將載板強⾏拉平，確保後續
⿈光微影製程的精準對位。
Source: TrendForce
2. Bump & Reﬂow （凸塊與迴銲）製程階段
貼合位置：  環氧樹脂（ EMC ）載體的背⾯。
作⽤機制：  當進⾏  Chip-ﬁrst 或  Chip-last 製程進⼊⾼溫迴銲爐時， EMC 與
晶⽚的膨脹率截然不同。此時  Balance Film 作為暫時性的應⼒平衡層貼合於
背⾯，可抵消⾼溫帶來的劇烈形變，防⽌晶⽚移位或焊點斷裂。
總結來說：  Balance Film 就像是拔河⽐賽中的另⼀⽅。正⾯（晶⽚與線路
端）有多⼤的⼒量拉扯導致基板變形，背⾯的  Balance Film 就必須透過精準

計算的材料特性（如模數、 CTE 等），施加同等的⼒量將其拉回⽔平。它是⼀
種具有功能性的暫時犧牲層，通常在製程完成、應⼒釋放或結構固定後，會透
過解膠（ De-taping ）程序移除。

### Page 7

Source: Andrew Lu on global semis/techs
Avoiding warpage to return
Balance Film 的主要⽬的不是「永久消除應⼒」，⽽是搶救製程良率。微影機
台的曝光對焦景深（ DOF ）通常只有數個微⽶的容忍度，只要基板翹曲超過極
限，光阻就會失焦、⾛線就會斷裂。 Balance Film 在關鍵幾道⾼溫化學品與
電鍍⼯站維持住絕對⽔平，其任務就算圓滿完成。
雖然從產業鏈了解，⽬前台積電交給專做  CoS chip on substrate 封測廠的
CoW AI GPU/TPU 在出廠時即使不使⽤  Balance ﬁlm 仍保持平整（部分原
因是矽中介層還沒超過 9 個光罩尺⼨），但時間⼀拉⻑，如果不貼上  Balance
ﬁlm ，也沒有其他結構接⼿，殘留應⼒必然會導致翹曲「彈性回跳」
（ Springback ）。所以台積電希望  CoS 封測廠能共同分攤 AI ⼤晶⽚  Balance
ﬁlm 隨產品出貨的成本。但在實際先進封裝產線中，翹曲之所以不會毀掉成

品，是因為隨產品出貨的  Balance Film 的核⼼使命是「階段性保駕護航」
—— 它確保基板在最脆弱的微影與線路成形階段維持平整，⼀旦進⼊後段，應
⼒的承載就會透過以下機制順利交棒：
1. 結構⽀撐接⼒（ Structural Handoﬀ ）
Balance Film 不會在基板毫無⽀撐的裸露狀態下被撕除。
通常是在晶圓 / ⾯板完成正⾯多層  RDL 製作後，先將正⾯與剛性載具（如
第⼆層暫時載板、 Bumping 載具）或次世代⽬標基板（如  ABF 載板）
完成貼合或鍵合（ Bonding ），隨後才將背⾯的  Balance Film 解膠剝
離。這時，結構的剛性已經由下游的承載體接⼿。
2. 加強框與散熱蓋物理鎖定（ Stiﬀener Ring & Lid ）
對於尺⼨超過  80mm × 80mm 以上的⼤型  AI 晶⽚，封裝完成移除製
程保護層後，本體仍有強烈的翹曲趨勢。

### Page 8

後段製程會⽴刻在封裝邊緣環氧樹脂表⾯貼上⾦屬加強框（ Stiﬀener
Ring ），或是封上均熱⽚ / 散熱蓋（ Lid ），利⽤⾦屬件極⾼的機械剛性，直
接將整個封裝體強制「鎖平」，避免在後續  SMT 焊接到主機板時發⽣焊
點虛焊或開裂。
3. 對稱性結構補償（ Symmetric Stack-up Design ）
Balance Film 解決的是「製程中單側堆疊引發的暫態應⼒失衡」。
在產品的終態設計中，⼯程師會在基板的上下兩側設計對稱的介電層
（ Dielectric Layer ）與等效銅箔⾛線密度（ Copper Ratio
Balancing ）。當封裝整體完成、所有⾼分⼦材料完全熟化（ Cure ）後，
本體正反⾯的⼒矩會互相抵消，使結構達到⾃發性的⼒學平衡（ Self-
balancing ）。
Story to be continued
在透過更多產業調研之後，我們之後打算出  Part II ，來聊聊
有沒有更優的防翹曲技術？
產業鏈核⼼競爭⼒⽐較？
如何測算  Balance ﬁlm 的市場規模及對營收貢獻的影響？

Document 378: US MARKETS CALL_ Invasion Of The Bond Vigilante Algorithms

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: US MARKETS CALL: Invasion Of The Bond Vigilante Algorithms
Date:    15 hours ago
US MARKETS CALL: Invasion Of The Bond Vigilante Algorithms

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
US MARKETS CALL: Invasion
Of The Bond Vigilante
Algorithms
By Ed Yardeni & Toby Hearst • 26 Sept 2026
View in browser

### Page 2

Photo by Boston Public Library / Unsplash
I. Credit
Last week's events confirm our "Proceed With Caution" call in September
15's QuickTakes. The main event was the jump in the 10-year US
Treasury yield above 5.00% to an intraday high of 5.22% on Friday
(chart). At the end of the day, it closed lower, at 5.18%, after news that

### Page 3

Iran proposed reopening the Strait of Hormuz and ending fighting in the
Middle East war. The Wall Street Journal subsequently reported that
President Donald Trump has told his staff privately that he’s skeptical that
Iran will meet his demands and that the US likely will launch a renewed
bombing campaign after the November midterm elections.
We suspect that the bond market has been hacked by Bond Vigilante
algorithms. They respond to news headlines with huge trades that

exacerbate bond market volatility (chart). The US Treasury bond volatility
index (a.k.a. MOVE) jumped sharply higher on Friday.
We also suspect that the unwinding of the yen-carry trade might explain
the worldwide uptrend in bond yields. Of course, central banks have also
been tightening in response to the inflation shock from the Middle East
war.

### Page 4

The credit market always sees trouble coming before the stock market
does—for example, the news Friday that a big data center project is being
halted.
Oracle, SoftBank, and OpenAI are tied together in the $500 billion
“Stargate” alliance; OpenAI provides the model demand, SoftBank
arranges the massive capital, and Oracle provides the cloud infrastructure
to train the next generation of frontier AI models. On Friday, Oracle issued
a force majeure notice on Project Jupiter, the flagship 2.45 GW New

Mexico data center, because the state denied the 17-mile gas pipeline
permit to power its Bloom Energy fuel cells. The banks that funded the
construction are stuck with more loans than they planned to hold for now.
II. Performance
So far, the stock market hasn't been troubled by these developments in
the credit market. Indeed, the Nasdaq rose to a new record high on
Tuesday of last week (chart).

### Page 5

The S&P 500 closed on Friday at 7743.41, only 0.7% below its August 13
record high (chart). Less reassuring, the equal-weight S&P 500 is down
5.2% over the same period. But it remains just above its 200-day moving
average.
Concerns about market concentration are back, as the Magnificent-7 have
outperformed the Impressive-493 since mid-August (chart).

### Page 6

The Russell 2000 is also down 7.6% from its record high on August 14,
though it is still above its 200-day moving average (chart). The index is
sensitive to interest rates and recession odds.
III. Earnings
Better-than-expected economic growth has also pushed bond yields
higher. Of course, a strong economy generates strong corporate earnings.
The forward EPS of the S&P 500 edged down last week from its record
high the week before (chart). We expect the uptrend to resume as

companies report Q3 earnings in October. The quarter's real GDP is
tracking at 5.0% saar, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of Atlanta’s
GDPNow model.

### Page 7

Industry analysts slightly lowered their EPS growth estimates for Q3 and
Q4 last week (chart). That's typical as a reporting season approaches.
Nevertheless, the uptrends in the forward EPS of the S&P 500, S&P 400,
and S&P 600 remain intact.

### Page 8

IV. Valuation
While S&P 500 forward earnings is up 28.3% ytd, the forward P/E is down
12.3% (chart). The index has gotten cheaper as earnings growth outpaced
the stock price index.
While the earnings outlook remains very strong, the valuation multiple
may continue to fall in response to the concerns we reviewed on
September 15 when we advised proceeding with caution (chart). That's
why we pushed our 8400 target for the S&P 500 from the end of this year

to the middle of next year. We are now targeting 7900 by the end of this
year.

### Page 9

V. Sentiment
The bull-bear ratios we follow are mixed (chart). In tandem, they are not
providing either a buy or a sell signal.

1
《人工智慧(AI)基礎設施公司融資需求和市值的成
長在近期投資銀行收入成長中發揮了核心作用》
高盛預期投資銀行三大業務領域均會實現成長 ，並
處於週期中期。在諮詢業務方面，預期未來12個月
併購交易量將成長 19%；股權資本市場(ECM)方面，
近期成長主要得益於強勁的科技業 (尤其是 AI相關
產業)活動，其中科技類 ECM貢獻了年初至今成長
的約75%，高盛預期成長將持續 ，估計2026年美
國IPO籌資額將達到 2,250億美元的年度新高紀錄；
而債權資本市場 (DCM)的增長主要會由 2大因素推
動，(1)AI相關融資規模龐大 ，其中相當一部分預

計將轉化為 DCM的業務量 ，(2)基於對企業併購交
易量持正向看法 ，這亦將為 DCM提供強大支撐 。
因併購活動中有很大一部份會依賴債務融資。
高盛預估今年迄今為止，併購、股權資本市場和債
權資本市場交易量及費用的合計增長中，約有

50~55%是由AI相關交易驅動的 。眼下Claude開發
商Anthropic何時上市就成為市場焦點 ，目前市場
推估最快10月中展開首次公開發行(IPO)的推介活動，
並可能在11月美國期中選舉前數日掛牌上市 。此案
被 視 為 史 上 規 模 最 大 的 IPO 案之一 ， 緊 接 在
Anthropic之後還會有OpenAI。
《市場預期股票市場成長依然強勁 ，固定收益 、外
匯及大宗商品(FICC)市場成長放緩》
銀行交易收入能否延續今年上半年強勁表現 ，其持
續性取決於市場水平 、融資利差和波動性 。從結構
上來看，可交易流通股數的增長帶動了交易量 ，進
而推升了客戶資金融通該持股所需的槓桿額度 ，通

常透過大宗經紀業務進行。
正向的週期性效益包括：股票融資利差是 2020年第
三季的三倍 ，反映出股票槓桿需求已超越產業產能；
首度公開發行(IPO)的回溫活絡了次級市場交易；個
股波動率升幅高於大盤指數波動率 ，為客戶創造更
大的超額報酬(Alpha)機會並提升參與度。故高盛預
期2026~2028年美國大型銀行股票市場複合年增長
率將達到 5~7%，但同期間 FICC市場的複合年增長
約為2~4%，這是因為市場波動性較低 ，限制了中
介活動。
《規模效應 、資產負債表彈性驅動資本市場收入步
入健康增長》
自 2019 年 以 來 資 本 市 場 收 入 加 速 成 長， 2019 至
2026年產業費用池複合年增率 (CAGR)將達 11%，
高盛預測 2026至2028年將放緩至 4%，雖說放緩但
仍維持健康增長 。這項成長主要歸功於可交易資產
池的擴張 ，以及全球債務和股權市場規模的擴大 ，
而非利潤率的提高，大型銀行交易資產自 2019年以
來成長約 110%，同期利潤率基本持平 。此外，自

2025年11月補充槓桿率 (SLR)改革實施以來 ，銀行
受惠於資產負債表更大的彈性 ，槓桿率 (平均資產 /
權益)在2019年到2026年上半年明顯上升 。同時，
經營槓桿上升帶來規模經濟效益 ，使資本市場業務
的效率比率 (營運成本/收入)下降，這是因為更高的
固定成本轉化為規模經濟效益 。最終，使資本市場
業務的股東權益報酬率(ROE)上升。

摘要：
以媒：客机返航不是因为劫机
据多家以⾊列媒体 9 ⽉ 30 ⽇报道，⼀架从阿联酋迪拜⻜往以⾊列特拉维夫的⺠航客机当天上午在
⻜⾏途中报告疑似安全事件，随后客机遭遇劫机的可能性被排除。
据以⾊列陆军电台报道，该客机发出与劫机企图有关的紧急代码。⻜机随后在约旦上空掉头，
最终在沙特阿拉伯紧急降落。
以⾊列安全部⻔消息⼈⼠随后透露，这架客机发出紧急信号， “ 显然是由于机上发⽣争执 ” 。以⾊
列媒体报道，此次客机返航 “ 不是因为劫机 ” 。
来源：新华社
⼀架从阿联酋⻜向以⾊列客机，途中突然异常改道，内塔尼亚胡紧急开
会，以军战机升空应对
据央视新闻，当地时间 30 ⽇获悉，⼀架从阿联酋迪拜⻜往以⾊列特拉维夫的商业客机当天在⻜
⾏途中，突然异常改道，⻜向沙特阿拉伯，并连续发出紧急遇险代码。
据悉，该航班⾪属迪拜航空，机上载有约 180 ⼈，于当地时间 7 时 05 分从迪拜起⻜，原计划于当
地时间 9 时 30 分左右降落在特拉维夫本古 - ⾥安国际机场。但在⻜⾏约⼀个半⼩时后，该航班在
约旦领空发出国际紧急遇险代码 7700 ，随后⼜发出代码 7500 ，在国际航空安全标准中， 7500 代
码意味着⻜机遭遇劫机或遭到⾮法⼲扰。随后，该航班转向沙特阿拉伯⽅向。
以⾊列总理内塔尼亚胡和国防部⻓卡茨等⼈⽴即召开紧急磋商会议。据安全部⻔消息⼈⼠透
露，⽬前尚⽆确凿证据表明发⽣劫机事件，⻜⾏员发出了求救信号，⽬前⽆法与⻜⾏员取得联
系。
据以⾊列安全部⻔消息⼈⼠透露，以空军战机已紧急升空采取应对措施。

Document 589: 宏观与政策周报2026年第36期（总第902期）：贷款降速提质将成为宏观新常态

Source PDF: all report pdf/宏观与政策周报2026年第36期（总第902期）：贷款降速提质将成为宏观新常态.pdf
Report source: 中证鹏元
Date: 2026-09-26
Pages: 13
Status: ok

### Page 1

主要内容：
 本周看点
9 月 16 日，中国人民银行行长潘功胜在《求是》发表《深刻认识中国
金融结构变迁 提升金融服务实体经济适配性》，系统阐述我国金融结构
变化及“十五五”时期政策方向。
一是融资结构持续调整，直接融资重要性明显提升。2013 年以前新增
间接融资占社融增量比重超过 80%，近年来直接融资占比持续提高，至
2026 年 8 月末，新增直接融资累计占比进一步升至 50.3%。与此同时，金
融资源更多投向科技创新、绿色发展和中小微企业等领域，金融体系的风
险承担主体也由银行逐步向债券投资者、股权资本和创投资金等多元主体
扩展。
二是金融总量增速趋缓与产业结构升级共同推动金融结构变迁。随着
社融、M2 和贷款存量持续扩大，叠加房地产、地方融资平台等传统融资
密集型领域收缩，信贷增速中枢有所下降，信贷“降速提质”可能成为新
的运行特征。与此同时，科技创新、高端制造等新兴产业轻资产、高研发
投入、长周期和高不确定性的特征，使其对股权资本、创投和耐心资本等
长期风险资本的需求上升，金融体系需要从单纯扩大资金供给转向提高资

金期限、风险属性与产业需求的匹配度。
三是金融政策框架相应由总量扩张转向价格调控、结构优化和多层次
市场建设。 货币政策将更加重视价格信号和利率传导，弱化对单一数量
指标的依赖；结构性政策继续围绕金融“五篇大文章”优化资源配置，注
重加强机制协同；同时通过发展股权市场、债券市场“科技板”、财富管
理和衍生品市场等，提升金融体系对不同类型、不同发展阶段企业的融资
和风险承接能力。
总体来看，随着经济发展阶段和产业结构变化，我国金融结构正从一
银行信贷为主逐步转向信贷、债权、股权等多元融资方式协同发展，金融
总量增速趋缓的同时，更加注重资金投向、融资效率和风险分担。与此相
适应，货币政策调控也将更加重视价格信号和结构优化，并提供发展多层
次金融市场，提升金融体系对科技创新等新兴产业的适配能力。
 宏观经济
贷款降速提质将成为宏观新常态
宏观与政策周报
2026 年 9 月 18 日

 上周利率走势分化，债市横盘震荡；

### Page 3

一、本周看点
【贷款降速提质将成为宏观新常态】
9 月 16 日，中国人民银行行长潘功胜在《求是》发表《深刻认识中国金融结构变迁 提升金融服务实体
经济适配性》，系统分析近年来我国金融结构发生的变化，并提出“十五五”时期进一步推动货币政策框架
转型、完善多层次金融市场和提高金融服务实体经济适配性。文章提出随着经济增长动能和产业结构变化，
我国金融体系正由过去以银行信用扩张为主的融资模式，逐步向信贷、债券、股权资本等多种融资方式协同
发展的方向调整。
当前中国融资结构正发生深刻变化，直接融资占比提高，资金更多投向重点领域与薄弱环节，金融体系
承担风险的方式开始发生变化。过去我国融资体系长期以银行贷款为主，2013 年以前新增间接融资占社融
增量的比重超过 80%；近年来直接融资占比持续提高，2025 年企业债券、政府债券和股票融资合计占社融
增量约 47%，首次超过贷款；截至 2026 年 6 月末，直接融资占社融存量提升至 1/3，贷款占比降至 60%左
右；至 2026 年 8 月末，新增直接融资累计占比首次超过 50%，达 50.3%。不过，当前直接融资占比上升更

多体现为债券融资增加，其中既有企业债券市场发展的影响，也受到政府债券净融资增加和专项债置换隐
性债务等因素推动，不能简单理解为股权融资已大幅替代银行贷款。同时，金融资源开始更多流向科技创
新、绿色发展和中小微企业等重点领域和薄弱环节。更值得关注的是，随着经济结构的变化，不同类型金融
工具在风险承担中的功能正在重新分工：银行信贷仍是金融体系的重要基础，而股权、创投等能够承担更高
不确定性的资本，在支持科技创新中的作用在不断上升。
金融结构变迁的根本原因，在于金融总量总体增速放缓以及实体经济转型进一步改变了融资需求。随着
经济由高速增长阶段转向高质量发展阶段，叠加社融、M2 和贷款存量已处于较高水平，金融总量增速自然
趋缓。目前房地产和地方融资平台的贷款余额占比仍较大，但比重在不断下降，2025 年至 2026 年上半年，
房地产贷款余额累计下降超 2 万亿元。这意味着其他领域的贷款要先补上下降的这部分，才能带来总量的
增长，全部信贷很难保持原来的增速，叠加当前内需修复动能仍然偏弱等因素，信贷降速提质更可能成为新

的运行特征。
实体经济方面，产业结构转型升级进一步推动金融结构变迁。过去房地产、基础设施和传统制造业投资
规模大、建设周期长，同时具有土地、厂房、设备等较充足的抵押物，更适合银行通过中长期贷款提供大规
模资金支持。据文章测算，房地产和交通运输业中长期贷款余额与行业增加值之比分别达到 1.7 和 3.3，意
味着行业每创造 1 元增加值，需要 1.7 元到 3.3 元的信贷投入。随着经济增长动能向科技创新、高端制造、
绿色低碳等新兴领域转换，企业资产更多体现为技术、数据、品牌和人力资本，融资需求呈现轻资产、高研
发投入、长周期和高不确定性等特点，据测算，信息服务业中长期贷款余额与增加值之比仅约 0.1。科技企
业在早期商业模式尚未成熟时，更依赖私募股权和创业投资，到成长中后期才逐步具备使用银行贷款、债券

### Page 4

等债务融资工具的条件。从这一角度看，当前金融领域面临的问题已经不仅是有没有足够的资金，而是资金
的期限、风险属性与新的产业结构能否匹配。创新活动具有较高的失败概率和更长的回报周期，如果主要依
赖要求按期还本付息的债务资本融资，容易产生期限和风险错配。因此，产业升级对金融体系提出的新要
求，是在保持银行体系融资功能的同时，增加能够容忍短期亏损、承担创新失败风险并分享长期成长收益的
资本供给。这也是股权投资、创投和耐心资本重要性提升的深层原因。
金融结构和融资需求的变化也要求金融政策框架同步调整。近日，央行印发了《中国人民银行“十五五”
改革发展规划》，顺应中国金融结构变迁趋势，持续优化金融结构，文章重点提出以下三点：
一是货币政策调控框架更加注重价格信号，弱化对单一数量指标的依赖。文章进一步提出“淡化对数量
目标的关注，特别是对贷款单一渠道的关注”，强调将金融总量更多作为观测性、参考性和预期性指标。同
时更加注重发挥价格型调控的作用，提出强化政策利率作用和健全市场化利率机制。这意味着未来货币政

策调控将更加关注资金价格是否合理以及利率传导是否顺畅，而不是单纯追求总量的增长。
二是金融资源配置更加突出结构导向，强调政策间协同配合。政策继续围绕“金融五篇大文章”优化资
金导向，同时强调结构性货币政策工具“聚焦重点、合理适度、有进有退”，并加强与财政、产业、融资担
保和风险分担机制的协同。在精准支持重大领域企业融资的基础上，通过增信、担保和风险分担等降低金融
机构单独承担风险的压力，提高资金与产业需求的匹配度。
三是推动现代化金融市场建立，为经济结构转型提供更多元的融资和风险承接渠道。政策明确提出“发
展股权市场和债券市场‘科技板’”，同时发展财富管理市场、完善利率和汇率衍生品市场、强化国债收益
率曲线基准作用，并继续完善金融市场基础设施和制度体系。多元化市场能够精准匹配各类型与发展阶段
企业的融资需求，尤其是对于研发周期长、商业前景不确定、缺乏传统抵押物的科技企业等，股权资本更能
够承担企业经营失败风险并分享长期成长收益，与银行信贷、债权融资形成互补。除此之外，文章还进一步
提出了健全宏观审慎管理体系，进一步要求增强风险防范的全面性和有效性。

总体来看，随着经济发展阶段和产业结构变化，我国金融结构正从以银行信贷为主逐步转向信贷、债
权、股权等多元融资方式协同发展，总量增速趋缓的同时，更加注重资金投向、融资效率和风险分担。与此
相适应，货币政策调控也将更加重视价格信号和结构优化，并提供发展多层次金融市场，提升金融体系对科
技创新等新兴产业的适配能力。

二、宏观经济
【8 月社会消费品零售总额同比增长 0.4%】
8 月社会消费品零售总额为 39,824 亿元，同比增长 0.4%，增速较 7 月回落 0.2 个百分点。其中，商品
零售同比增长 0.3%，较上月回落 0.2 个百分点；餐饮收入同比增长 1.1%，较上月回落 0.3 个百分点。分城
乡看，城镇消费品零售额同比增长 0.2%，较上月回落 0.3 个百分点；乡村消费品零售额同比增长 1.6%，与
上月持平，乡村消费表现仍相对较强。限额以上单位商品零售额同比下降 4.0%，降幅较上月扩大 0.2 个百
分点，反映消费恢复动能仍偏弱。从环比增速来看，8 月社会消费品零售总额环比由微涨转为下跌 0.13%，
连续 7 个月低于季节性。
从分项看，消费表现继续分化。8 月通讯器材类同比增长 27.3%，较上月加快 6.9 个百分点，烟酒类增
长 12.5%，文化办公用品类、饮料类、化妆品类和中西药品类分别增长 5.8%、4.9%、4.9%和 4.8%；食品类

同比增长 4.0%，较上月收窄 1.3 个百分点，连续保持正增长，说明刚需消费仍保持韧性。家用电器和音像
器材类同比增长 2.3%，较上月的-1.9%由负转正，部分受到基数走低的影响。另一方面，汽车类同比下降
18.5%，降幅较上月扩大 1.5 个百分点；金银珠宝类下降 17.5%，降幅明显扩大；建筑及装潢材料类、家具
类和石油及制品类分别下降 11.8%、7.9%和 7.0%。1-8 月，社会消费商品和服务零售总额同比增长 2.5%，
其中服务零售额增长 4.9%，商品零售额增长 1.0%，表明服务消费需求仍相对旺盛。
整体来看，8 月社零增速延续 6 月以来的放缓趋势，商品和餐饮消费均有所降温。其中通讯器材等部分
品类保持较高增速，食品、办公用品等刚性需求仍具有韧性；但汽车、金银珠宝及家具建材等大宗可选消费
仍构成明显拖累，消费内生修复动力仍有待增强。目前居民对长期收入和就业仍信心不足，“以旧换新”政

### Page 7

策虽仍有支撑，但对社零总量的边际拉动有所分化。后续随着国补边际退坡、房地产相关消费仍受制于地产
下行，年内商品消费修复空间或相对有限，服务消费将成为政策主要发力方向和消费增长的重要支撑
图表 4 8 月社会消费品零售总额同比增长 0.4%，较上月回落 0.2 个百分点

三、重要资讯、政策监管回顾
1．国务院部署加强中小企业回款难治理，进一步规范账期管理和资金传导
近日，国务院办公厅印发了《关于加强中小企业回款难问题治理有关工作的通知》，并于 9 月 14 日
召开政策例行吹风会介绍相关情况。
《通知》提出了 4 方面 10 条具体措施：一是健全行业账款支付规则。提出分行业明确合理账期，结
合行业实际制定完善结算支付管理规定，明确“付款四项关键要素”；要求发布大型企业及时支付倡议，
推动龙头企业率先践行“60 日付现承诺”。二是加强大型企业支付行为监管。提出对故意拉长账期的大型
企业开展联合约谈整治；对滥用竞争优势地位的大型企业严格反不正当竞争执法；完善细化上市公司和大

型企业等账款披露要求；发挥央企国企及时支付的表率作用。三是规范管理非现金支付工具。提出规范管
理电子凭证业务，将电子凭证最长付款期限压减至 6 个月；从严监管电子凭证服务平台，要求平台依法合
规运行。四是强化资金传导效能和融资支持。提出畅通资金传导链条；支持大型企业融资置换应付账款，
及时向上下游中小企业支付现金。
2．国务院：研究算力网建设有关工作，系统推进老旧水库改造
9 月 11 日，国务院总理李强主持召开国务院常务会议，研究算力网建设有关工作。

### Page 8

会议指出，算力网是人工智能发展的基础支撑。要坚持合理布局、规范有序，进一步完善算力基础设
施，积极推进关键技术和装备研发应用，构建多层次网络化算力体系。要推动算电协同、算网融合，加强
算力资源监测调度和骨干光纤网络建设，强化算电设施规划衔接，加快绿电直连、源网荷储等项目落地。
要更好发挥市场机制作用，支持企业开展科技创新、资源整合和应用推广，促进供需更好匹配。
会议指出，水库安全关系防汛抗洪、江河治理和经济社会发展大局，要坚持长短结合、标本兼治、分类
施策，严格落实各级责任，全面提高老旧水库安全能力。要增强老旧水库改造提升的针对性有效性，科学优
化改造标准，“一库一策”制定改造方案，严把工程质量关，同步提升调度管理、日常管护水平，筑牢水库
安全防线。要加强部门协同和要素保障，吸引更多社会资本参与。
3. 发改委：围绕优化发展环境、拓展投资空间听取意见建议
9 月 15 日，国家发展改革委主任郑栅洁主持召开民营企业座谈会，围绕优化发展环境、拓展投资空间
听取意见建议。
与会企业结合经营现状、行业发展和投资计划，就进一步开放应用场景、优化审批流程、拓宽融资渠

道、盘活存量资产、强化要素保障、健全标准体系等方面提出意见建议。
郑栅洁表示，民间投资是民营经济高质量发展的重要环节，是扩大有效投资的有力支撑。希望广大民
营企业家把握机遇、创新求进，在新兴产业、新型基础设施、消费升级等领域积极加大投资布局，促进新
旧动能转换，更好服务经济社会发展大局。国家发展改革委将深入贯彻落实党中央和国务院决策部署，聚
焦民营企业投资意愿强、成长空间大的重点行业领域，采取有针对性的务实举措，增强民营企业获得感，
推动民间资本放心投、投得动、多参与。同时，不断完善与民营企业常态化沟通交流机制，分行业分领域
常态化推介优质投资项目，有序开放重点领域应用场景，有力促进硬投资和软建设紧密结合，持续优化投
资环境、拓展投资渠道，多措并举促进民营经济高质量发展。
4. 发改委：公开推介 36 个重点领域项目，激发民间投资活力
9 月 10 日，国家发展改革委民营局主要负责同志主持召开有关民营企业和行业协会座谈会。
会上围绕民营企业投资意向，集中推介交通、物流、水利、能源等四大重点领域 36 个项目，预计总

投资 614 亿元，拟引入民间资本 156 亿元，项目类型涵盖铁路、仓储、风电、储能、充电设施、引水工程
等，民营资本可通过独资、控股、参股项目公司等方式参与。
下一步，国家发展改革委将充分发挥与民营企业常态化沟通交流和问题解决机制作用，会同有关方面持
续优化服务保障，强化资金、土地等要素供给，协调解决重点问题，全力推动项目早落地早实施。同时，建
立项目常态化推介机制，拓展民营企业参与优质项目投资机会，更好促进民间投资发展。

### Page 9

5. 中评协就公募 REITs 资产评估指导意见公开征求意见
9 月 14 日，中国资产评估协会发布《基础设施领域公募不动产投资信托基金（REITs）资产评估指导
意见（征求意见稿）》，对外征求意见，进一步规范项目估值标准。
意见首先明确了公募 REITs 资产评估的三类业务场景，包括基金上市注册评估、基金存续期间年度评
估，以及项目购入或出售、基金扩募、资产处置、现金流重大变化等其他情形评估。在此基础上，征求意
见稿进一步区分所有权类和经营权类项目，其中仓储物流、园区、租赁住房等所有权类项目主要评估基础
设施不动产，交通、能源、市政等经营权类项目主要评估经营权权益。考虑到公募 REITs 以获取稳定现金
流为核心特征，原则上采用收益法评估，并分别对租金、空置率、运营成本、收益期限以及经营收入、资
本性支出、折现率等关键参数提出具体要求。同时，文件强化评估信息披露要求，要求披露重要评估假设
和参数、经营权到期后的资产移交安排，并可通过敏感性分析进行风险提示；附件还针对园区、消费基础
设施、保障性租赁住房、收费公路、能源、市政和生态环保等不同资产类型列明差异化评估关注点。

整体来看，征求意见稿从评估业务场景、评估对象、方法参数及信息披露等方面进一步统一公募 REITs
资产评估标准，有助于提升不同项目估值的规范性、透明度和可比性，为市场持续扩容和资产类型多元化提
供制度支撑。
四、货币市场
1. 上周流动性紧张，回购利率全线上行
上周流动性紧张，回购利率全线上行。具体来看，隔夜利率较前一周上行 5.28BP 至 1.42%，7 天回购
利率上行 1.66BP 至 1.42%。中等期限方面，14 天回购利率上行 1.83BP 至 1.44%，21 天回购利率上行
1.9BP 至 1.43%，1 个月回购利率上行 2.95BP 至 1.45%。

2. 上周利率走势分化，债市横盘震荡
上周(2026/9/7-2026/9/13)央行 7 天逆回购投放 85 亿元，到期 100 亿元，合计净回笼 15 亿元。上周债市
整体延续横盘震荡，短端小幅下行，中长端普遍上行。其中，1 年期下行 0.45BP，5 年期、10 年期、30 年
期分别上行 2.12BP、0.95BP、1.25BP。周初受 3000 亿元特别国债注资等因素影响，市场对流动性预期阶段
性宽松；但随后由于缺乏利多催化，叠加资金收紧，出口数据偏强、通胀小幅回升，市场对通胀抬升担忧再
起，带动中长端利率小幅上行。后续关注油价走势对通胀影响，以及财政金融协同促内需增量政策出台的可
能性。
